About this text

The Lexical Treatise called Clear Illumination of the Manifold, also named The Pearl Garland, known in Sanskrit as the Abhidhānaśāstra Viśvalocana, otherwise called the Muktāvalī, and in Tibetan as Mngon brjod kyi bstan bcos sna tshogs gsal ba, is a Sanskrit dictionary of synonyms and words with several meanings, composed in verse by the Indian poet and paṇḍita Śrīdharasena. After an homage to the guru and to Mañjughoṣa and an invocation of Sarasvatī, goddess of learning and speech, it sets out its vocabulary in sections, listing the many words that name a single thing and the many senses a single word can carry, and closes with a mixed section of assorted terms. Works of this kind served poets, scholars and translators as tools for mastering the rich vocabulary of Sanskrit literature. It is Toh 4453 in the Tengyur, where it belongs among the treatises on language and lexicography.

Translation

Herein is contained the lexical treatise called Clear Illumination of the Manifold, also named The Pearl Garland.

In the language of India: abhidhāna śāstra biśvalocana mitya sarābhidhāna muktyabalī nāma

In the language of Tibet: The Lexical Treatise called Clear Illumination of the Manifold, also named The Pearl Garland.

Homage to the guru and to Mañjughoṣa.

May the Blessed One and his Dharma be victorious! Sarasvatī, be wholly gracious!
Bring happiness to beings, and lead them straight, away from arrogance and nonvirtue!
And this water, too, that flows from the supreme, well-fashioned vase of mine,
Whatever it may offer, may those skilled in meaning enter into it entirely.

For the sake of its sections, all of it, glory devoted to courage:
There arose a glorious master of poetry called Munisena.
My field of learning, with its words and with fine poetry,
Is gathered from all the renowned treatises.

Therefore glorious Śrīdhara, who has seen to the far shore of the whole nature of speech,
Who became a vessel for the steadfast minds of the guides who dwell on earth,
Composed this, the very drink of nectar of all the supreme poets,
Endowed with the immortal Sarasvatī.

Steadfast, born on the path of eminent poets,
Made by one with the eye of intellect and the eye the guru has taught,
Drawn from the lexicons composed by various lords of poets,
This work, which is like an eye, is like a treasury of lamps.

It fully illumines the steadfast scriptures of poets and of logicians,
It dwells securely in the city of the sciences,
And it is blended with the paths of thought of the eloquent;
So now, through it, even the words of the ignoble will be overcome.

All learning, whether made easy by effort or not,
Firmly applied to the minds of the powerful holders of learning:
Here, again casting off conceit, this stainless jewel Kaustubha
Rests in this vast work, like the glory of the goddess of fortune.

In the midst of the ocean, the treasury where the lord of the nāgas dwells,
Born from the oyster shells that are the mouths of various lords of poets,
Strung by the wise Amara on a magically made silken thread,
Place this well-arranged pearl garland firmly in your heart.

The garland of flowers of renown,
With the fine fragrance of freedom from attachment:
Whoever abides at its feet by attending upon it
Becomes like the nāga tree.

Those skilled in grammar, those who hold the lexicons, and worldly people,
When they see it even today, it gives rise to great joy in their minds.
Therefore this effort of mine will be shown above all for the sake of others;
I have made it to crush the arrogance of opponents, for the sake of joy.

Whatever fruit the learned of old
Intended for words,
Here, following the systems of Amara,
I set out the words with certainty.

Heaven, the inexhaustible, the excellent abode,
The threefold base, the firmament, and the sky,
That which comes from taste, the blissful, the sky,
The arising of fruits, the holder of leisure,
The abode of the gods, the all-born,
The sorrowless, the realm of the Thirty, heaven,
And the high sky: these are thirty separate names. The abode of Indra,
The happy destiny, the delight maker, nabha, the playground of the gods, the land of enjoyment,
The upper rodsī, the world, the city of the gods,
The world of the gods, sairika,
tabiṣa and tābiṣ,
The summit of Meru, and the abode in the sky.

binasvanta, the one with celestial palaces,

prācīna, apacīnaṃ,

udīcīnaṃ, apācīnaṃ:
for the east and the rest, these three denote the directions themselves.
prāka, and apāka, pratyak, and so on,
are not limited to place, direction or time.
Where the direction itself is expressed, the endings marked above and below
give the three, east, south and the rest, as the directions themselves.
pracyā, apācya, pratīcya,
and likewise udīcya, are the three for the directions themselves.

Eighteen blinks are a kaṣṭha;
thirty of those are called a digit;
thirty of those are a moment;
twelve of those are a muhūrta;
thirty of those make a day and night.
Fifteen of those are a fortnight;
the former and latter fortnights,
the bright and the dark, those two, are a month.
Mārgaśīrṣa and the rest, two months each, are a season;
three of those are a passage of the sun.
The year has two passages, southern and northern,
and has two times when day and night are equal;
these, from the order of the sun's transit,
are to be known in the Ram and the Balance.

Niṣāda is like the sound of a flute.
Dhaivata is like the neigh of a horse.
Gāndhāra rings like the bleat of a goat.
Ṣaḍja is like the cry of the peacock.
Ṛṣabha is like the lowing of a bull.
Madhyama is like the call of the crane.
Pañcama, one, is like the cry
of the peacock in the season of flowers.
When all of these are sounded in intoxication,
that too is pañcama.
kākali is a subtle sound.
Indistinct and sweet is kala.
A deep sound is mantra.
Very high is tāra.

are called bāti and tramāta.

Twelve.
The fierce, and what is called the violent, and

terror, fright, anxiety, and
being defeated, and fear.
The changes of the mind produced
by joy, dejection, anger and the like
are experience and the recognized change.
That experience, in turn, becomes
of the nature of complete enjoyment and of attainment.
There, what arises from the four eyes,
the raising of the brows and the circling of smiles,
vivid experiences and the like,
are held to be of the nature of complete enjoyment.
Longing, likewise thought, and
recollection are held as a group;
likewise weariness, and raving,
madness and illness itself,
and what is called jabha, the tamer, too:
these are the ten of the nature of attainment.
Laughter is what causes joy;
likewise both are delight.
What are called concealment and wonder
are proclaimed as the setting forth of states.
Through the consciousness that brings under control,
the action born on the path of things:
the knot of the mind is egotism;
the proud mind is elevation.

These are the divisions of the ocean and the rest. The section on the netherworld is complete. From the Abhidhāna Viśvalocana, otherwise called the Pearl Garland, composed by the master of poetry, the glorious paṇḍita Śrīdharasena, the first chapter is complete.

the auspicious and the all-good,
the beautifully colored and the increasing,
the joy-coil and the rest;
others again are divided by the arrangement of their doors and so on,
with special designs and lights set among them.
Of the palace of the mighty,
other particulars too become gifts and the like.
The queens' house, of the one who enjoys the earth,
the inner one, the palace with guards,
the one with pure borders, the all-enclosed. Four.
The pinnacle and the mansion,
the upper roof and the enemy house.
Indra's sound and aṃgaka,
mandaninī, the cattle pen.
For the enclosure of the finest elephants:
kutraṃga and urghaṭa,
and what is called siṭharā.
satmī, kanyāṭaniḥkuṭī.
The great road between the towns
is called rācchā; the market house
and the place of wares are the same.
The small turret, cayabapri. Two.
The iron wall, the shelter, the hard to pass,
the arrow enclosure is its foundation;
the wall, the long moat, the rim at the edge,
and what is called the road of talk. Six.
The surroundings, the circuit at the edge of a town and the like,
bound with reeds, wicker and the like;
the protection, the circuit, the built-up and stacked,
and what is called eḍukaṃ.
Within it bones and teeth are set.
The wall, bhitti, kuṭyaṃ.
The embodied one's house and the all-holder.
The doorway, the door-front, the door-base:
that is the courtyard of the outer gate.
The street and the side of the house. Five.
The covering, the roof, the covered cāla. Four.
kuṭīla and nīgra,
the covered, the garlanded edge,

and what is called śā. Four.

that same is called muḍḍha.
One with flowers and fruit is grove-born.
A tree established as a shrine is firm.
A tree's shade is the shelter it shows.
A short-rooted branching one is kṣupa, the shrub.

The uncommon grass that grows,

This is the chapter on medicines.

So ends, from the Pearl Garland, the section on forests and medicines.

the well-housed, muṇḍaka, the yellow one,
the cut-impaired and the full,
the other-delighting, the sesame-oiled, garodghādhī, the fruit,
the melodious, the one graceful in the sky,
and what is called the bright-voiced.
Then the curd-bearer, the curd-host,
and what is called the curd's abode.
Phabaṃ, the leather bird, bhadkārī,
the leather-winged, jatunāṃ,
the house-goer and the crow's enemy.
Bāguli and bagu,
taruhu and kāraṭabyāṃ,
dhanacchakā, the moon-entering,
the captive-bodied, the crow, the round-headed goer.
Particular kinds of bird: hārīta,
kāraṇḍa, vartakā,
tunikā and spoṭikā
are alike. Further, the moth,
chagapa and the fire-carrier,
the darkness-destroyer, the light of the sky's rim,
the sky-glow and the light of the sky,
the darkness-dispeller, the path-light, the seed of light,
and those called saḍārbu.
The blue dung beetle, makṣikā,
the honeybee and the honey-born.
The ant is puttikā.
The very small honey bee is sarsdu.
The fly's bee, the kinds of bee:
vikaṃ, daṃśa, bhambhaḍā,
the forest bee, bhiraḍī. Four.
The biting insect and its other kinds:
varāṭī and gandholi,
garolli and brāhmī,
barṭā and the poison-horned,
bhṛṅgaḍola, paḍala,
and the grass-cutters.

Bhṛṅgaḍī and jhīrukā,
cīrī and jhillikā are alike.
The fly-winged and the singer,
the melody-maker, the sound-maker, and the buzzer too,
the slipper, the water-goer, the trunk worm,
its kind, the srarbaka,
the once-insect and the other-maker,
the timely, bhakṣarāṭikṣa,
the black-eyed and the worm,
ussika and the sun-flower.
Feathers, wings, quills,
the coverer, the leaf, the body-born.
The shoulder, the wing-root, pakṣati,
the row of wing feathers, ariñchalī; flying and going.
Flying forth and flying high,
flying well: all these
are the actions of the sky-goers.
The hollow, the treasury, the sheath, the egg. Four.
The beak, the cutter, the close-grasper. Three.
Offspring, body, limb,
the newborn, the child, the young and the chick,
the little pot, the fledgling. Seven.
Male and female together, the two-two,
the pair, the couple, the matched,
the twofold, the second, the twins,
the brace, and what is called the set. Eight.
Group, the like-purposed, the general gathering,
assembly, aggregate, congregation,
accumulation and pile,
the full assembly, the collection, the multitude,
the gathering, the troop, the net-born,
bṛnda, maṇḍala, kudumba,
nikara and stoma,
visara and prāsta, sammā and saṃnaya,
khaṇḍa and samudaya,
kṣatvau and vaṭayaṃ,
saṃhati and nivaha,
nicaya and cakra,
cakravālaṃ, kalāpa,
viḍaka and prakara,
sarikara and yūthaṃ as well.
Pāśa, except with hair, means a group,
and so do grāma and the like, with something before them.
The divisions of a group, section and the like, are alike.
Of the same kind and of like nature,
of that kind and similar,
the assembled, the like-purposed, the kin-born.
For animals it is herd and flock of cattle;
for the others it becomes gathering.
Some, of like purpose, are the group of groups:
heap, stūpa, pile, mass,
and stack. A flock of doves,
a flock of parrots, a flock of peacocks,
a flock of partridges and so on: these are their flocks.
Household birds and wild animals,
and some of the rest of these, should be included.
This is the chapter on birds and the rest.
The section on lions and the rest is complete.

Homage to the Buddha. Man, the mind-born, the son of Manu, the knowing, the minded, the strong one. Six. The born, the five-born, the person, the protector, and the male. Five. Female, the woman, the bounded lady, the lady, yoṣā, yoṣita, the binder, the side-glancer, the left-hand lady, the ground of joy, the honored lady, the powerless, pāyā, the fierce lady, the strong, the delighting lady, the left-acting lady, the abiding lady, the bearer, the girl, the lady of essence, the well-delighting lady, the harm-guarding. Twelve. The divided lady, the delighting lady, the gladdening lady, the desiring lady, the left lady, the excellent lady, the guiding lady, the eyed lady, the embodied lady and, for some, the timid lady, the meditating lady, the broad-hipped lady, the much-intoxicated, the beautiful lady, the lovely lady: these are the twelve ladies. The angering lady, the terrifying lady, the fierce lady and krodhanā: these are alike. Four. The supreme, the well-born, the bright lady, the supreme creeper, the intoxicated bright lady. Five. The clever lady and the jointed lady. Two. The young woman and taluni, the lady of passionate age, the young, the wealthy, the lady who takes sides, the quarrelsome lady. Seven. The lady in her prime, the town-holder, the intoxicated lady, the bright lady, the empowered one made queen, the lady of enjoyments, the lady of the lord of men. The embodied lady and the lady with a lord, the one taken by the hand, the second lady, the lady with Dharma, the wife, the one who moves within the home, kalatraṃ, the binder, the one who binds most firmly, the holding lady and the bearing lady, the burden-bearing lady, the companion. Twenty-two. The betrothed, the new to the house, the bride, the one of first menses and the husband-chooser, the self-chooser and the reconciled, sarābanni, suta, pautāyuka, the serving lady. The lady of the family, the family-guarding lady, the one with a single lord, the truthful lady, the well-conducted, the lady vowed to her lord: these are the good women. Then, the one whose menses have not come, the fair one, the naked one. Three. The maiden, the band of girls, the lady who delights in the dark, the boon-giving lady, and navapatikā. The lady with a messenger, the one turned from sight, the lady with menses, the rain-bearing lady, the lady who has seen menses, the girl, the one with essence, the one in the interval, the newly placed, the one of the excellent new, new menses, gold-sowing, new flower, the flower-seen, the interval lady. Twenty. One who has a little youth and stays in her father's house is the fixed-dwelling lady. The housewife, the town-holder, the lady with children, the bearer, the delighting lady, the bride, the daughter-in-law, the one sounding the sweet lute, the sure proclaimer, its lord, sagaṅga, dābisyāda and parati. Seven. The fickle lady, the harlot, the independent lady, the binding lady, the truthful lady, the sweeping lady, the signaler, of a portion of the clan's gathering, the lady garlanded with intoxicated ways, dubyaścaṅgī, the signing lady, the bond-giver and the portion-maker. Eleven. The desiring lady, bhojana dhanadiṣicchābasa batīkā, the desirous lady, priṣasyāndī tukāmithunecchāratyambakī kāntvanvinītuyāyāte saṃgetaṃ sābhisārikā. One. bādhīnabhartrikāsyata tyaktvañaṃ yadpaśaḥsriyayāsukalpititelpasñisundarī pasabaśmānimatrapakapajjabiyasmāgamotribiprālandhāsamayabyāṃ dutīkāpreṣyayatmataḥ barāegaṃkrītasaṃ ketoreḥ namaṃlatipriya. Four. yābadhirmapriyakopātacāṭukarañcanirgatātīsyata nuśayeḥ pañcatkalahantaritāhipā. Five. utakiṇṭhatātupāyasyābāpa beśmaniśāgataḥ priyonāgamanehentubintuṃ yantipamākulaṃ. Six. kutaścitakārṇatayasyaḥ priyaudśāntaṃraṃgatakṛtvabadhīṭinaṃpārtāproṣītapreyapībet. Seven. kutaścannagatikānte the all-tormented lady, the broken lady, the beautiful lady, the delighting lady, the undertaking lady, the lady of purified eyes. She is also called the ground of the delighting lady and the stake of joy. Viprṛktā and the ill-fated lady, the one who has borne seven sons, bskarā, the naked woman, the clothless lady, the lady with sight and the child-gainer, the youth-snared lady, the unsupported lady, the unheroic lady and the lady of corresponding cause, the lady each her own, the lady with a follower, the ground of action and the displaying lady, the minded lady, the lady with a net.

The son's son and the rest as well.

The tip of the foot and prabadaṃ. Two.
The ankle, ghūṭika, gumba,
the heel, the back, goḍari,
and what is called the root of the foot. Seven.
The calf and phraprita,
baṣṭhī and badaṣṭhībān,
the joint near the knee.
The thigh and pakthi; its junction
is paṃkṣaṇa and pāsuraṃ.
The excretory path, the lower door, the downward path, the buttocks,
tanū, hra and the lower path,
the excretory door, dṛti, paṇī. Nine.
Below the navel, the crotch, the waist,
śroṇi, kakurmati, the waist. Three.
The waist, the girdle region,
the girdle-fruit, karihaṃ,
the golden place and the binding,
kaṭī, protha, sphiṭa.
Of women's hips,
the back part is nitamba,
the front part is called jaghana,
and the support of the back is drikaṃ.
Its door: bhaga, the place of birth,
and mastaka. The cheek of the buttocks,
nitambastho, the all-touched, below the navel,
kūbituṇḍi and

the birth-holder, the hole of joy,
the flower path, the maker of existence,
the son's abode, the village, delusion,
the deluder and the intoxicator. Twenty-two.
The sign, medha, mehana,
the near abode, śeba, the unaccomplished,
the lier, the pleasure-maker, the pestle,
the intoxicator, the hook, haṅgulaṃ,
the pillar of mindfulness, the intoxicating vine-tree,
the mark, the clinging vine, the banner. Thirty-five.
The scrotum, the egg, the treasury and the sheath,
the treasury of fruit, and the navel.
The belly, jaṭhara,
the flank, and what is called tunda.
The chest-born, the breast, the milk-holder,
kucam, the side-born, the mark of youth,
the nipple, the nipple tip. The front itself,
and the back, the rear of the body. Four.
The joined, the all-joined,
the middle and the between.
The chest, the breast hollow, the upper thorax,
koḍi, koḍā, between the arms.
The heart region, the seat of life, the vital point.
The shoulder, the arm, the part of the head;
their junction is jatruṇī.
The two roots of the arms are the armpits;
below them are the ribs.
The actor, the descender, the enterer, the arm.
The elbow, the middle joint, kapaṇi.
Above the elbow is the upper arm,
below the elbow is the forearm,
which is called kilāṭī.
The root of the hand is the jewel-binding.
The end of the hand is karluṣe.
The hand, the maker of conventions,
the branch, the fivefold, the hand,
the elephant's trunk-like, the leaf of the arm. Six.
From the wrist to the little finger
is karabho, the edge of the hand.
The fingers are the branches of the hand.
The thumb is the eldest of the fingers.
The pointer is the index finger.
The middle finger and the ring finger,
the little finger, the youngest.
The rest are explained as the nameless.
The nail, the regrowing, the claw,
the intoxicator, the hook, the hand's hero,
the hand-sprung, the hand-born, the hand's joint,
kharu, ila, the hand's arrow,
the acting thorn, karaṭa. Twelve.
The pointer's span and the cow's nose
are the measures with the index finger and the rest.
The span from thumb to little finger
is called twelve fingers.
The palm, the slap, pratila,
is the palm with the fingers extended.
Saṃhatala is the two together.
Pratala is right and left.
A hand slightly cupped is a palmful;
the two joined are a double handful.
The hand stretched out is called a cubit;
with the fist clenched it is the short cubit.
Arala is with the little finger extended;
its variegated form is with the fist clenched.
A fathom is the arms together with the hands,
spread out to the sides.

With the arms stretched upward,
it is the measure of a man, the man's height.
Vyāyāg and viyāma,
the arm-bow and the body's fathom.
The Adam's apple and the throat.
The neck, the head-stay, the head-holder,
the head-support, the conch-like neck with its three lines,
kākalaṃ and the throat-jewel:
these are called "the vase-like."
The nape and the hollow of the nape.
Mouth, face, the speaker,
lips, countenance, the door of utterance.
The smeller, the scent-descender, the nostril, the nose,
ghoṇā and nāsika,
nāsikyi and śilbi,
nasyā, nikraṃ, gandhanāsikā.
Upper lip, lower lip, the tooth-veil,
the garment of the teeth, the tooth-binder,
the essence-taster, the petal of speech, the tooth-holder.
Below that is the chin,
cacūkama and the desirer.
Tooth, the cutter, the palate-row, the jaw-row,

the fang, the thruster, the twice-born.
Gargo, the cheek, kapola;
beyond it is the jaw.
The tongue, the nectar-dripper,

the mother of essence, rasanā,
talanana, koku.
The end of the lips is the corner of the mouth.
The support of the mouth and of nectar, honey,
the palate, and kāku: these are the same.
Kurcca is the space between the brows,
and above the eye is the eyebrow.
The seer, the guide, the eye, the eye of the noble,
cakṣu, the sense faculty,
tṛga, the gaze, the lamp of the gods,
clear sight, ambakaṃ.
Tears, eye-water, vapor, the stirred,
moisture, the dripping, that which arises on occasion.
The eyelashes are the house of the eye's jewel.
Apāṅga is the corner of the eye.
The fruit of the eye, the seeing eye.
For a look from the corner of the eye:
the sidelong eye, the side-glance, the half-look.
Ear, the grasper of sound, the abode of sound,
the hearer, the listener, hearing,
śrotraṃ and śrota.
Forehead, the ornamented one, bhāla.
The holder of the eyes, the supreme limb, and kaṃ,
the head, the crown, the brain-bearer,
muṇḍa, śirṣa, inexhaustible bliss.
Hair, the coiled, the regrower,
kaca, that which lies on the head, the head-born,
the discarded, the crown-born,

kurala, the godlike,

the topknot, the hair-cord, kaṭira,
the ascetic's matted locks, jhaṭa.
The parting of the hair, and byaṃ,
ornament, and the like of coiled locks:
these are the bees of the forehead,
crow's wings, the crested,
the braid, the hair-endowed, the plait of hair,
matted locks, the fine mass of hair.
Cord, side and hand,
placed after the word for hair, mean a mass.
The body-born, body hair and the hair of the pores;
when these have grown, they are beard and moustache.
The beard: the cherished, together with the rounded head.
Body hair, guccha, the dappled one.
This is the chapter on human beings and the rest.

The illuminating, the making-visible, the making-appear,
adorned and thoroughly adorned,
decked with ornaments, the ornamenting,
completed, fully accomplished and decorated,
beautified, bound with ornaments, nabathyama,
separately crafted and made beautiful,
the act of adorning, become adorned,

fully dressed, and the ornament of the maṇḍala.
Diadem and head ornament,
small crest and kiriṭa.
The crest jewel, the gem of the head,
the jewel of the crown, the crown gem.
The gem set in the middle of a necklace, tarala,
is the same as "the leader."
The hair-cord and the hair ornament,
the crest-hook and the forehead ornament.
Conch, the sandal-cutter,
the making-visible, and the forehead-bearer.
The ear's pride-maker, tāḍaṅga,
earring and ear-wrap,
tālapaṭa, the palm-leaf,
the stamened one, the ear ornament,
the ear-loop, the ear-string.
The bee's throat and the girdle,
the neck ornament, the throated, the peacock one,

the wearer of the throat ornament, and svanta,
lalantikā, the long-hanging,
the pearl garland, and saiboḍ,
the pearl string, the intermediate necklace.
A single strand, wound once,
that same becomes the garland of the lunar mansions,
having twenty-seven pearls.
The guccha is wound thirty-two times;
wound twenty-four times, it is the half guccha.
That which is wound sixty-four times
is the half necklace.
Twenty and sixty
are the māṇavaka and the breast-string.
Made with four windings, it is the seven-god;
that which is made with eight windings
is the seven-victory.
The necklace is wound eight times eight.
Five hundred is the mass of light rays.
The coiled oyster, the conch's whorl,
the hand-goer, the twice-drinker, that born in the mountain ranges:
from these arise the kinds of pearl,
but from the oyster above all they arise.
Bangle and bracelet,
kaṭakaṃ and the wristlet.
Hand ornament, finger ring, the fingered;
one with waves and bearing letters
is called the signet ring;
aṅchaccheni is the same.
Armlet and upper-arm ornament.
A golden bracelet is kaṃkaṇa.
The hand-thread, the finger-follower, karantu.
A woman's girdle: the waistband,
the tasteful, rasanā, the golden,
the clustered, the sevenfold, kakṣya.
A man's girdle, and the chain.

mañjara and the foot ring,
the goose-like and the foot circle,
the little bells and the tinkling one,
the gem of noble women, the foot-cord,
and the toe ring: these are the names.
Bark, the worms of insects,
and hair are the sources of cloth.
Cotton cloth comes from the fruit of the cotton plant;
woolen cloth is born from the hair of animals;
silk comes from the cocoons of worms;
linen from flax and the like.
New cloth and the unworn,
the new garment, the delightful cloth, and garment.
What is called udgāmanīya
is a pair of washed garments.
Leaf-wool and washed cotton cloth.
The costly and the greatly valuable.
Dukūla cloth is the illness-dispeller.
The garment worn and the garment in use.
Clothing, covering, robe,
the guard against nakedness, the wrap, aṅgaśa,

state, and cloths.

The loin wrap and the lower wrap.
The lower garment of noble women
is the caṇḍataka garment.

Cloth, the turban, nāraṅgi,
are called "the all-wound."
A garment that reaches to the tips of the feet
is called "the sturdy and comfortable."
The wrap worn above
is the two, sillī and ucchalikā.
Fine cloth is dukūla,
naktakaṃ and karbaṭa,
the frost garment and the calf-pattern.
A garment that guards against snow and wind:
here cloth, aṃkuśa,
and what is called añcala are also accepted.
Long in length is āroha;
wide in breadth is viśāla.
The lower garment, the conch-like,
nībī, and likewise varti.
For women, for the most part,
it is of ten cubits, and twofold.
Substance and viśikā,
the gathered and śālūra.
A blanket is red woolen cloth.
Worn under the armpit and worn at the side,
the garment worn: these are two.
Canopy and upper covering,
tent, and what is called the awning,
curtain and screening cover,
the concealer and the cloth covering.
That same is the cloth covering, the curtain cloth,
tent, the hair tent, the dwelling house,
the robe-dwelling, the cloth house, that made of cloth,
the pass-holder, the playful one,
the khrang house, and the abode of qualities.
Full grooming, the dressing up of the body,

Consecration, the thorough bath,

The tumba-seeming and the bird's entry,

Finger-leaf, letter-leaf, leaf,

Musk, the fragrant-robed,

Saffron, the hero, ghṛsriṇi,

Aguru, the pleaser of kings,

crow-face and yellow sandal,

Black aguru is aguru;
the auspicious garland, fragrance,

Sisanturaṃ, the ear-holder,

That which bears the names of curd, milk and butter,
the garment of glory, and pine resin,

Yakṣa incense and white incense,

Lac, lac dye, jatu,
dye, the tree-dweller, yāba,
nirbhatbanaṃ, the mother of dyes,
and the one possessing the finest color: these are its names.
Camphor, musk, aguru
and kakkola make the sacrificial paste.
That which has passed from place to place,
the seventh, is piśāca incense.
A careful preparation of scents, garlands and the like
is the preparation.
Well bound on the brow, with delight:
that is the flower garland of the head.
Made to look as though it fills the ear,
that likewise is the finest, the ear ornament.
The fragrant maruvaka
strung as a garland is tama.
The flower garland is of the crown;
in the middle of the hair, the essence-bearer.
Hanging above, hanging at the tip,
hanging in front, lalāpakobo.
The long-hanging hangs straight from the neck.
The flower garland worn crosswise, the sash,
is that which is bound as a garland upon the chest.
The fixed garland and the stainless are the same.
Clove, the flower of the gods,
the substance of the gods, and the one named "glory."
Striving severally, the composer of explanations,
the tamer, kacanārṭhaka,
the former, the actual, worship, the effortful,
the effortless, and the fully complete.
Bed, sleeping place, place of slumber,
couch, and lying down.
Row, cross-legged, the cross-legged seat,

Cushion, small seat, stool,
pillow, the support, the leaned upon,

What is called asigaṇḍa,
kola, ganḍu, bāṭukā,
and what is called gallacāturi.
Scented powder, malanaṃ,
būg, sājala,
and what is called pharukaṃ.
Basket and casket,
the copper basin and the spittoon,

praṇṭho, kaṭakolākā.
Out of season it becomes būga powder;
out of season, all mixed together.
Tooth powder, śakemasrabaṃ,
ibhikā and guṇḍaka,
and it becomes kaṭaśriṅgala.
The mountain-born and kānhvakā,
the inciter and guḍa.
Lamp, the blazing, the oil-eater,
the wick-drawer, the one fed by the wick,

the smoke-maker, the lover of oil,
those with blazing locks.
The bee, the viewer, the eye-salve.
Then the kaumudī-stand,
the lamp-stand and the crest-stand.
As for the seat of the learned:
throne, seat and cushion.
Comb and fine-toothed comb,
hair implements, the cleaning of the hair.
Rubbed in, applied to the cloth,
made fragrant, and gone stale,
niścukkaṇaṃ, softened and stilled.
The fan and the cooling fan.
Mirror, the all-seeing, mūkura,
mukura and karkaraṃ.

Thus, in the Clear Light on All Things, also called the Pearl Garland, arranged by the master of poetry and prosody, the learned Śrīdharasena, the section on human beings is complete.

Lineage, family and descent,
origin, the earlier-born, the follower,
family, clan, the line of descent,
and what is called "its line."
Born of a line, born of a family, arisen from a family,
the well-regarded, and sprung from a family.
For one arisen from a royal family:
the one with royal seed, the royal line.
The great family, the good family, the noble family,
the supreme family: these are excellent persons.
The four castes are the brahmins and the rest.
The twice-born, the twice-born, the eminent,
son of Brahmā and brahmin,
pāḍapa, the mouth-born,
the gū-born, the one of second birth,
alaya, the throat-bodied,
the eldest of the castes, the bannered one,
yujānaśca, the speaker,
the mare-born, the one of the crossroads,
the mighty, the reciter, and birth.
Brahmin, and born of Brahmā.
For the wives of brahmins:
brahmin woman and noble lady.
Born from the extreme of purity,
brahmin and son of Brahmā.
His six actions are performing sacrifice,
reciting, giving and the rest.
The brahmin and the twice-born castes,
the royal caste, the merchant caste, the common caste:
their births are called
tarīya and datayātama.
The celibate student, the one who keeps the law,
the forest dweller and the monk:
these are the four stations.
The well-born, the celibate, and the learned are the same.
The maker of the learned, and the kūba-eyed.
Then the householder, the elder,
the settled, the master of the house, the holder,
the home-minded and the home-knower.
The forest dweller, the homeless, vāikhana,
the monk and the wanderer,
the giver of acts, bārasāri,
maskaḍī and the well-tending dweller,
the fully desiring, austerity,
the ascetic, the vow-keeper,
the wanderer and the fully guarded,
one who endures the afflictions of austerity, the disciplined,
the sage, and the one restrained in speech.
The far-seeing and the long-seeing,
the wise, the realized, the learned,
the steadfast, the knower of minds, the one possessed of wisdom,
the knower of faults, the all-knowing, the poet,
the well-born, the intelligent, the one of fine intellect,
the hero, the famed, the illustrious,
the supreme intellect, the understanding one, the skilled,
the numerate and the clear:
the door of clarity and the one with the letters of the times
are called the teacher of letters.
One who sets others to recite becomes the preceptor.
One who performs consecrations and so forth is the guru.
The student is the attendant, the disciple,
the doer of tasks, the novice,
the permitted, the family, the one with the door-panel,
one who understands the training, the beginner,
the thoughtful one, the poet,
the one at the end of words, the knower of treatises.
An obstruction is the boundary, baya, pratyuha.
Byanāya and śīla,
habituation to virtues, firm application,
explanation and commentary are the same.
One who explains the secret mantra is the master.
One who holds to local custom is the vow-keeper.
The sacrificer is the performer of sacrifice.
The drinker of the moon-drink is dīkāta.
One with many sacrifices has performed sacrifice.
Yājā is the sacrificer who longs for heaven.
One who has offered the sacrifice of Jupiter is sthapati.
The drinker of the moon's liquor is somapaḥ.
One who has sacrificed with all the Vedas
is worthy of offerings in all the heavens.
Anucana is one well spoken,
who has learned the Veda together with its limbs.
One who has received leave by the word of the guru
is called samābṛtta.
Sutā is one who has taken the ritual bath.
One who keeps the vow of Brahmā alone,
and the other, who keeps the vow of Brahmā alike:
satīrṭhyā are those with one guru.
Prabaṃ andabāda:
the unaccompanied becomes the performer of fire offerings.
The fire that is heaped up is idhacir.
The instruction of the lineage is aitikṣaṃ,
the true instruction fully bestowed
through the succession of the lineage of gurus.
Close knowledge and first knowledge;
composing after knowing is the near approach.
Burning through sacrifice is adhvaḍa.
Sacrifice and the seven-threaded,
makha, kratu, saṃstara,
the row, the continuity, the act of scripture,
the water of ease, and the canopy.
The royal sacrifice, the arranged,
the adhara of the lord of men.
Recitation, the fire offering, honoring guests who come,
offerings to the ancestors, and the torma:
these are the five great sacrifices.
The sacrifice to Brahmā is reciting aloud;
the sacrifice to the gods is the fire offering;
the sacrifice to humans is honoring guests who come;
the sacrifice to the forefathers is satisfying the ancestors;
the sacrifice to the bhūtas is called the torma.
Assembly, the surrounding circle, gathering, meeting,
samiti and sipa,
samiti and saṃmijya,
the all-dwelling and sarda,
nana, and the gathered.
House, the first line,
the first, the fire offering, the gathering,
the sudden gathering, tāra,
sabhāsad, the frequenter,
the gathered, and the observer of rites.
The knower of meter and the learned are the same:
śauti, śrotiyatā.
Whoever has caste alone and a livelihood
is said to become a brahmin in name.
Adhvasūd, bhrahri,
the performer of the fire offering, the sacrifice,
by the order of verses and Vedas,
outside the agnīdhi and the rest:
the seasonal priest who carries out the Veda.
The sacrifice of the moon's course
and the vow of Indra are the same.
That is the samstāpa sacrifice.
The ground of praise, secluded from people,
the Vedic ground, thoroughly purified,
the level plain and the crossroads.
For the hearth of the fire offering: tara,
mahārī, the fire of taste, makhā.
The smoke of the fire offering is the fixed joint.
The ash of the fire offering is beṣṭataṃ.
The life-post to which the victim is tied is caṣāla;
the vase is the house fully encircled;
the top of the life-post is tarma.
The fire-drill, the rubbing base, araṇi.
The southern fire, the household fire,
and havani: these are the three fires.
These three fires are tetā.
Driṇata is the well-purified fire.
Samṛkṣaṃ is fully used.
The fire of close application is well joined.
That which belongs to the householder is dānīma.
The southern fire is praṇiya;
that becomes ālasya.
Then the meters, gāyatrī,
uṣṭīka and the rest as well.
What these speak of, the kindling sticks and the substances burned,
is called the treasure of fire.
Amikṣā sāpritoṣṭi
is milk mixed with curd.
The cooked offering is catu,
the finest food and milk porridge.
Habya is the food of the gods, made with milk;
kabya is the food offered to the ancestors.
For these, made with milk:
sūṣabyin and the one with sūṣa.
The fan and the cooling fan
are made from the hide of deer.
Ladles are distinguished as the filling ladle, the pouring ladle, and so on.
The pouring ladle is bradhaba, the holder.
Srukarṭhāhū performs the fire offering.
The spoon is what stirs the butter.

Upakṛta is the animal
bound to be slain for the sacrifice,
handled on every side and secretly sprinkled:
it is the beast of the measure for slaughter.
Pramīta bears the mark of what is to be spoken.
Upasampanna is one slain after being sprinkled and given to drink.
The substance burned, given in the fire offering,
furnished as food, is what is made paṣaṭa.
Those two are one: the god of the sacrifice.
The substance of the fire offering is bānnammā.
Then the sage is one who has cut off speech.
The one ending with dikṣī is the chief of the sacrifice.
Those who do the work of slaying are yajñiyaṃ.
The act of sacrifice is the desired, and pūrta;
digging wells and the like are its acts,
the acts of the desired, of pūrta, and so on.
Amṛta and pighasa
are the eating of what is left from the sacrifice;
as for the substances left from the sacrifice,
the vessel-gift and the distribution.
Offering the torma, giving,
handing out and giving away,
bestowed, dispensed, spread abroad,

the offering, the fee, and flowers.
Giving for the sake of the dead
is called aujadehika.
The gift to a dead father is bānebasa.
Wood-food; beyond that, the gift of treatises.
The one with the noble, alms and the like.

The price of worship is the guest offering;
for the sake of the feet, water is the cooling of the feet.
The guest, the sudden one, the newcomer,
and the well-born: these are the names.
Going, wandering, going again,
walking, and going.
Touching water and rinsing the mouth,
gaṇḍuṣa and filling the mouth.
Gradual, sequence,
complete order and following order,

The vow, the fast, the meritorious,
thorough restoration and abiding near,
the fast, the one that frees from all sides,

Keeping the monthly fast,
eating at the end of one, at the end of three,
the sixth meal and the timely,
the place of sacrifice and the steadfast in sacrifice,
the solitary, the separate self,
the singer and the reciter of the Veda.
Reciting the Veda of Brahmā
and the one accomplished at night are named.
Brahmā and birali.
The spittle sprayed while reciting
is called the drop of Brahmā.
Generating and reciting the Veda
arise from the source of Brahmā.
The quality is sparmasatika.
There too is the high limb.
Meditative concentration, the seat of yoga,
the seat of Brahmā, the ordered rite of realization,
the chief and the realization,
the subsequent rule, the subsequent decision.
Taking up the formative rites beforehand
is the close approach, and scripture.
Celibacy is the restraint of all the senses:
the one with senses tamed, peaceful in the village,
the peaceful, the one striving in restraint, the restrained,
the one of great vows, yobhiṅga,
the abode of purity, the one under the vow of bathing.
Whoever, by force of a vow,
sleeps on the bare ground, sleeps naked, is sthaṇḍila.
Then, free from attachment, tamapa,
free from afflictions, beyond the pairs,
the sign and the conduct, possessed of a garland of Dharma,
the clear display of vows.
The younger brother who in his folly
takes a bride before his elder:
he is to be known as paribeta;
paribe is his elder brother.
Bībaha is the unimpaired.
Born of a vow, the one who abandons the formative rites,
all who keep the vetāla vow,
those who make clear and those who torment,
the insubstantial and dunihā,
the renouncer, the swift-goer, the celibate,

and the bearer of every sign: these three are the same.
The practitioner and the deliverer,

clean, pure, stainless,
merit, and the clear-minded.
The holder of the sālāśa staff,

The alms-seeker and the beggar,

His are meditative concentration, its possessor and its doer,
recitation and repetition.
Bathed, bath water, saba:
by the Veda and all repetitions
one destroys evil.
On the fifteenth, when the moon is seen, and at the new moon,
one performs the fire offering at the end of each fortnight.
Whatever act always depends
on effort of the body is exertion.
The constant act is the true gathering.
One haste is ṭuṃkata.
What is fixed is the constant act;
for the occasional, one performs service.
Then the remainder becomes the purifier.
The sacrifice is sarībābata.
Upabīta is the thread
of the sacrifice, borne by the twice-born.

Prārcīnāpīta is wound on the left.
Nibāta hangs at the neck.

The line of ash on the forehead,
the three charcoal lines, and tiryaka.

is bhāla, ba, and the three.

at the root of the thumb one gives the water of Brahmā.
Brahmā reborn, Brahmā himself,
the one truly endowed with Brahmā,
and likewise the births of gods and the rest.
Hardship, austerity and the like.
Going without a bed is bhābhyana.
The knowable, the view, freed by bhida,
bhyuditotrau, in due order,
sanbyasabatyān,
sanephere, gains and the like,
the mover, the skill-born, hypocrisy,
and the deeds of the hypocrite.
The three groups are Dharma, desire and wealth;
the fourth group is liberation.
That fourfold power is the supreme path
to the city of Brahmā.

Speakers of truth, speakers of blessings, singers of praise:
in council, these are the great sages.
The brahmin sages are Vasiṣṭha and the rest;
those who made the scriptures and so on are the sages of scripture and the rest.
Atri, the Leaf, and the rest are the royal sages;
and there are also the sages of the occasion,
Jaimini and the rest, the knowers of the Veda.

Where di is hidden is yatinaṃ;
that is held to be manorataṃ.
The solitary, the place without people, the hidden,

these six are called "secret."
Upīśu and embracing,

For words of excellence: lion,
tiger, śarabha, bull,

expert, white lotus and the like are spoken;
placed after "man," they express the chief.

Thus, in the Clear Light on All Things, the Pearl Garland, arranged by the paṇḍita, the master of poetry, the glorious Śrīdharasena, the section on brahmins is complete.

The lord, the minister, the trusted ally,
the treasury, the realm, the fortress and the army
are the two limbs of the king.
Those called the capital and the palace,
peace, war, and setting out,
staying put, giving wealth, and relying on another:
these six are called the qualities.
Capacity and might,
enthusiasm and the recitation of counsel;
decline, stability and growth:
know these as the three sets.
Overpowering, manly might,
splendor, radiance and strength,
force and the majestic,
and the storehouse filled with wealth.
Division, winning over, conciliation,
and exacting tribute: the methods are of four kinds.
Tax, levy and assessment;
slander and stirring up are the same;
then division and distribution.
Upadha is that which closely examines
by means of the Dharma and the like.
What six eyes see,
what is the domain of three or more.
The equal, the clever, the trusted.
If fallen, the lowly; how one goes about,
the unfallen, the reasonable, the examiner,
the one who shows form, and the one who abides as equal.
Reason, method and attainment,
the fitting, the proper, the customary,
the rightly obtained, and "in place" are said.
A commission to someone not compelled:
that is individual instruction.
Instruction by it, complete instruction,
commissioned instruction, advice, teaching,
hard instruction and permission.
Law and holding.
Convention and local custom,
the right grasp, complete binding,
definite seizure, restraint and binding are the same.
Offense, fault, the culpable,
tuṇḍikā and the one who goes by family descent.
For two and more, the penalty is taken twofold.
Revenue, tribute, the hard-to-collect,
what is obtained as tax: toll.
Its wealth, kunva,
the present and the enjoyment,
goods, lañca, ukau,

Elephants, horses, chariots and foot soldiers
are the four divisions of the army.
The one with the reversed tongue, the one born of a she-elephant,

One that is hard to tame with the goad:

The she-elephant: that is the scent elephant,

It is brought under control in an instant.
If marked, bhāsto.
The radiant one, and the sacrifice's
supreme elephant, and the one bearing the royal ensign, are the same.
Bitaṃ is a common elephant.
A herd of elephants is an assembly of elephants.
The animal-given and the one to be tethered,

Below the frontal globe is bāhaṇyaṃ;
pratima is below that.
The one with globes, the two elephant bulges;

Ārakta is the edge of the globe;

The rear of the shin and of the tip
are aparaṃ and yogita;
the front of the shin and the portion of the tip
become gātra; the root of the tooth,
the root of the tail, and its end.

The mouth part of the full one is padmakaṃ;

and then giving is the rut-fluid.
For the elephant's ornament: śrimagāṭa;

hakko and garjja are calls.

Then pṛṣā and kakṣā;

The discerning, the excellent person,

Horse, the water-drinker, the swift-goer,

Good horses have five of each:

Mallikā: those whose eyes are not white,
black, and like Indra's bow.
The winged one, the pure horse for the sacrifice,

The wind's horse and the region of the earth,

Kaṭhyan is the horse's waist.
Heṣā and hāeṣā are its neigh.
Trotting, going in the four directions,

The horse's face and muzzle.
The mane and the side of the mane.

Horse-tail and tail,

The horse's bit and kabamaṭyaṃ,

Vehicle, the one that goes on wheels,

Vehicle, chariot, the conveyor,

Charioteer and leader,

The arrangement of the troops

The back of the army is the reserve.
One elephant, one chariot,

is called padātikā.
By tripling each component,

Excellence, prosperity, glory and good fortune.
Poverty, lack, the absence of excellence.
Slowly one becomes a great protector of the earth.
The one at leisure, weapon, balpari,

Arrow, the path-possessing, śāyaka,

is called the chief of weapons.
For the tip: the seeker,

The poisoned: lista, the arrow-side,

The shield is called phalka.
The leather one, the straight sword,

Hammer, the crusher, ghana.

Battle-axe, axe, the cutter,

The cutter, gzod, the knife, the curved knife,

Spear, the long weapon, the rope-spear,

The short spear and kāśrī are the same.
The throwing spear, the finger-spear, pirśaṅga,
the spear, and the one called the boundaried.

The trident and the three-bordered,
stake, the firm one, śaṃku,
the peaceful and the flower-part: these are the same.
The tip is called koṭaya.
The edge. Weapons are of four kinds:
released from the hand, released by a machine,
muktā, puka, muktakaṃ.
Released from the hand: the śakti and the like;
released: the water-knife and the like;
muktāmukta: the iron bar and the like;
released by a machine: the arrow and the like.
The arrow-guard, for the long arrow,
the surely outward, and baṭṭiśam.
Staff, iron bar, cudgel,
the discus and the other kinds of weapon.
Practice with arrows and weapons is
ubāpani, khuralī.
The king consecrates the army;
against the enemy the force goes first;
whoever goes to war receives the consecration.
Counterattacking and advancing back,
marching to battle, and utterly blocking.
All-out fighting and conflict,
destroying the army and surely entering,
the march to war, setting out, departing,
entering, going, gala.
Entering and staying there,
scouting the land is held to be pratiratha.
Stretching out and the extended troop,
for the purpose of entering the enemy's army.
Baitālikā, the half-armored,
the one who brings about well-being,
the one with a wheel and the one with a bell,
the hard-to-equal and the one with bees: these are the same.
The naked one, the one whose vow is praise,
the one who pays homage, goraga, the reciter of a thousand,
the one who recites sūtras at dawn,
the singer and the one who pronounces auspicious words,
the enjoyer, the mighty one, the one who offers homage and praise.
Victory, the victorious, the victor.
Dust and kṣoda.
Close fighting and engaging.
The melee and the partyless.
The earlier holding and worship,
the host of enjoyments and completeness itself.
Banner, Vaijayanta,
the victory banner, the finial, the ear of the sky,
kadhalī and kardhalī,
the gathered, the signifier, of bhaja,
abacūla, ucchula,
the increase-giver, kha, and kurbaka.
Charnel ground, the battleground,
the giver of utter terror.
Saying "I first, I first!"
and what is called "I am first":
the remarkable one, the arrogant man,
that is the proud one and the egoist.
Saying "Me, me!" and vying with one another:
that is rivalry.
The forceful, the outstanding, the bold,
the strong, the brave, the fearless,
destroyer, might and diligence,
capability, life-force, and overpowering the foe,
overpowering, and great bravery.
One who destroys in the place of harm,
the supreme hero, is the one who
drinks liquor before and after the battle
and wins the fight he engages in.
Saṃkṣa, mṛdhi, salīka,
the many-maker and kaṃdanaṃ,
utter destruction,
fighting in battle and contention,
saṃyuga and kan, great destruction,
the formations of battle, the great contention,
the seizing, and what is born of battle;
the unhappy, engaging in battle, ajini,
is called samiyudha.
When in battle, by fighting, both sides
die together: anaṃka;
the inauspicious is held to be saṃspoṭa.
Sāmbarayiki and
the manifest itself and complete destruction:
these are tumults in general.
The many-maker is the terrible fight.
Battle without weapons is śatumula;
fighting with the arms is yurha.
The sound of the elephant's frontal globes,
the cry, fighting, and the increase of all enemies,
the elephant's trumpet, the lion's roar,
the war drum, paṭa, the sound of ha,
are the same; taraṭṭa and
the manifest substance and araṭṭa,
and the proud gargara.
The hero's effort, the hero's nature,
the arrogant undertaking, maṭakaṭi.
Deceit, guile, the evil method: naṭha.
Then confusion and error,
the killer, the one entering death, the dead,
the one surely seized, the one turned back again.
Fainted, stupefied, swooning,
tormented and afflicted.
Wounded, impaired in body, fallen.
Enmity, sport, and retaliation,
the one who bears a grudge and the one in a blood feud.
The frightened, bholo, the faint-hearted,
is called paṇḍagaṭa.
Fled, scattered, fled close by,
utterly fled and fled in all directions,
gone back and turned back,
fled everywhere, and routed.
The one with jackals, the fanged,
formations and wandering about,
the night of time, the terrifier, the chariot-lady.
Defeated in battle, the other's victory,
defeat and the opponent's victory,
hidden and protected.
Killing without remainder, striking surely,
killing and beating,
pounding, and utterly destroying,
overpowering and penetrating,
harming, wholly abandoning, rendering homeless,
without clothes, without seat, without home,
caka, and destroying wealth,
sure harm, sure injury, sure removal,
displacing, dragging away from elsewhere,
removing, and covetousness,
niśuntana, sure torment,
great destruction, tormenting and torment,
the all-obtaining, the going, the utter churning,
destroying, cooking, harming,
the harmer, the goer, injuring,
condition, casting away, entering into full measure,
striking, churning, entering into complete measure,
wasting and pacifying,
destroying, sure removal, injuring:
these become what is called its production.
Gone to sleep, the night, awakened, dead,
earth, fire and water,
the equal and the one with fruit,
the state of the five, the law of time,
the end of sight and destruction,
passing beyond and final dissolution,
going far, setting out, resting completely,
the state of the five, the end, decay,
dying, death, setting,
falling surely, and reaching the earth.
Corpse, the one who has reached the state of the five,
the deceased, the one who has arrived, the one laid out,
gone elsewhere, dissolved, at rest,
the slain, and gone elsewhere,
and what is called the tenth.
Corpse, the heap, the one gone to its place,
corpse-flesh, the earth's increase, the five states.
The one come to the end, bārutha,
the bier and bāṭi,
the corpse's chariot, the wooden strongman.
Kabandha and the active one
are the trunk without the head.
Charnel ground and the forest of the ancestors,
the playground of the Fierce One, the place of peace,
the place of destruction, the burning place, the place of pretas,
the corpse-hearth, the egg-cave, cati,
and what is called the place of wood.
The painted one and the caitya,
the corpse-mound ever heaped up.
Well done, honoring, treating as excellent,
and making supreme.
Seized, captured, to be held,
the one who bows, and mati.
The prison, the house of binding,
the jail and the house of punishment,
the wooden place, the wooden house, the punished and the bound.
A person's life is prāṇa;
likewise living and alive;
the lifespan is the time for which one lives;
the man's sustainer, the medicine of life.
This is the section on the limbs of the army.

Thus concludes the section on the royal class, from Clear Light on All Things, also called Pearl Garland, composed by the master poet, the paṇḍita, the glorious Śrīdharasena.

Thigh-sprung, thigh-born,
jau, and the merchant class,
the earth-holder, and the lesser lord.
Livelihood, and harming,
the farmer's living by cultivation,
that which sustains, and that which engages.
People, the world, the creatures,
the farmer, the herdsman, and the trader:
these are called the dharma of livelihood.
Service, engaging, view,
speaking the truth, and field work,
truth, and shipo ñca,
plowing, digging, and turning the furrow.
The unbegged and amṛta;
the begged and mṛta.
Prāmitya is living by interest.
Debt and parya,
borrowing and lending,
exchange and turnover.
Giving back in return and barter,
fixed exchange,
trade, exchange, another share,
and the lending out of wealth,
the idle, and one who lives by interest.
Mālākṣikāha,
raṇidhāva, truth and untruth.
Viśan is giving a loan;
interest, and the increase of wealth.
The holder and the lender;
uttama is the chief lender.

The sages' treatise on all dharmā,
the scripture of remembrance, and the treatise.
Number, crest, and measure,
the two texts, two and three, the one with ten letters.
Inference and the one with scripture,
the reason, pervasion, the sign, and the proof.
Then cause and substantial cause,
condition and cooperating factor.
Reading and familiarity with it,
compendium and practice.
Concordant with the cause and resembling it,
kunda, the Pleiades, the teller of time.
Intention and conduct.
Wheel and maṇḍala.
śunḍanaṃ and moving.
kṣoraṃ is also making good.
The feathered, and whatever does that,
the supreme, and whatever torments.
By the rāmāyaṇa of Rāma:
proclaiming sound, bringing to exhaustion.
Entity, the meaning of a word, phenomenon,
sentient being, gone, and conduct.
Casting the aspect of the past,
what must be established, many letters.
Suchness and its instant,
word, and also "principal."
Boy and uncompounded,
obscuring and unsupported.
Whatever does, and doing individually,
each itself carried through to the end.
Thorough etymology, the subsequent manner,
together with sāntānaṃ: these three are alike.
Whatever is radiant, path,
principal itself, and light entering.
Then water, breadth, and place,
height, and "having form."
Wholly enjoying and wholly destroying,
wholly longing and wholly holding,
wholly correcting and wholly making,
wholly reviling and wholly covering.
Portion, birth, poison, weapon,
the one to be killed, and wild beasts.
Fitting, appearance, destruction,
thick, and turned to dregs are expressed.
lāṭi, petals, heads,
place, district, settled abode.
Portion, near word, scent and food,
binding and searching,
the property of the Buddha and the wealth of a stupa,
the property of sentient beings, and kin.
By the number six, in the word for cattle,
it is taken as "six cows."
Two kinds, dispute, and form,
doing, the migrator, joy,
virtue, misdeed, written on the heart.
Thought, mind, and done in the proper way.
Turning the wheel, and weeding,
certain destruction and not going.
Cluster of villages, village, and sweeping,
the chaffy, wealth, and the joyful.
Merchant and merchandise,
attending closely and casting aside.
ruṇḍa, body, binding, vessel, and abode.
Site, livelihood, and drink,
heart, and skin, are held to be down.
ala, a hundred, and the place of bowing:
here they are not established thus.
Deceiving, and the meadow,
garland, row, and entering in rows.
Holding all, and the water vessel.
Stupa, and the gate of the guide.
Direct perception, and seeing the object clearly.
Hero, subduing, and overpowering.
Then extolling and undertaking,
and also "turned toward it."
Born of one's own longing, and bowing,
born of agitation, and as connection.
siṇo, the water's baraṇḍa,
pakura, and abiding truly.
Protecting all, merchandise, bowing,
golden vase, boundary, and the nosed one,
full, accomplishing, supreme bowing.
Destroyer and aṣṭaka.
Elephant, drum, and great bath,
Great Brahmā, hearing, and porridge.
ābalyaka utkaṇṭha.
Minister, intermediary, two meetings.
Frog and cow killer.
Food and making, tarkuṭa.
Day, macukaṃ, neuter.
māmā jyiti belongs to horses.

This completes the mixed section of Clear Light on All Things, also called Pearl Garland, composed by the master poet, the paṇḍita, the glorious Śrīdharasena.

Translator's notes

This is a Sanskrit synonym lexicon (in the tradition of Amarasiṃha's Amarakośa, which the author names as his model); most entries are Tibetan calques of Sanskrit synonyms, rendered here by their literal sense, and many Sanskrit words transcribed in Tibetan letters are visibly corrupt in the Tibetan, so they are given as spelled. The Tibetan has small displacements: the count "thirty names" for heaven and "fifty-two names" for the gods stand inside the lists rather than at their ends, and are kept where they occur. The opening verses are very compressed and in places obscure (notably the end of the first verse, the first line of the second, and the close of the sixth); they are rendered as literally as sense allows. Munisena and Śrīdhara (the author Śrīdharasena) are identified from the Tibetan thub pa'i sde and dpal 'dzin. This is a synonym lexicon whose Tibetan is interrupted by interlinear headings ("N names for X") and Sanskrit glosses that have been copied into the middle of verse lines (for example "Twenty-three names for Avalokiteśvara" splits "Of Many Forms," and "Eight names for Marīcī" splits "the Youth and the Crest-Bearer"); they are translated where they stand. Several running counts do not match the items listed (thirteen for the Avalokiteśvara list announced as twenty-three; three after a pair of names); the numbers are given as written. Uncertain renderings: "the One of Ten, the Twelve-Eyed"; "Lord of Kings" (literally "lord of earth-sustainers"); "The Devadattas" in the Buddha list; "His son, shown as one of perfect enjoyment"; "The crest: Devadatta"; "Delighted by the Maiden"; "Lion-Fierce"; "the Beautiful Destroyer"; and the closing three epithets, which seem to begin a list completed in the next piece. As in the preceding pieces, interlinear headings and Sanskrit glosses have been copied into the verse lines; they are translated where they stand, and running counts are given as written even where they do not fit. "Six names for the monk" reads "dge slong la med drug", where med is evidently a slip for ming, "name". In "His father is Vasudeva", nor lha (elsewhere in this text the Vasu gods) here names Kṛṣṇa's father, so Vasudeva is used. Many epithets in the Viṣṇu and Balarāma lists cannot be matched with confidence to a Sanskrit original and are rendered literally, notably "the Hand-Like", "the Ivory-Born", "Son of the Woman Dull of Hearing", "the Half-Text", "He of the Net amid the Rubbish Heaps", "the Ripened, the Faithful, Yama", "Hand of the Ender", "Maker of the Lord of the Earth" and "the Unclimbing". "The Delighter" after the bow probably stands for Viṣṇu's sword Nandaka. As before, interlinear headings and glosses are embedded in the verse lines and are translated where they stand; running counts are given as written even where they disagree with the headings (the Kāma list is headed eighty-nine but closes with twenty-nine). In the Śiva list, ʼphrog byed, used of Viṣṇu above as Hari, is rendered Hara, its sense when applied to Śiva, and rnam ʼjigs is rendered "the Terrifying", since here it is an epithet of Śiva rather than the name Vibhīṣaṇa. The lines "This is judged to be unconnected" and "from the eight the All-Destroyer, worthy, worthy, the Victor is also he" appear to be scribal or interlinear remarks copied into the text. A number of epithets could not be identified with a Sanskrit original and are rendered literally, among them "the Mountain-Dull", "Ground of the Withered Body", "He Named with I", "the Fish-Jointed", "the One Who Moves from Where", "the Rotting-Bodied" and "He Who Speaks at the Boundaries". The verse giving the lunar mansion, night and moment of the Destroyer of Beings' birth is rendered literally; its names are uncertain. As in the earlier pieces, interlinear headings (such as "Ninety-three names of Īśvara's wife" and "Seventeen names for Gaṇapati") and stray words have been copied into the verse; they are translated where they stand. In the Gaṇapati verse the words "the Aging Lady" and "Head of the Fierce Lady" fall between the syllables of tshogs ... bdag (Gaṇapati), so they are marked as standing between. Grol bum is taken as a variant of grul bum, kumbhāṇḍa. In the list of the Goddess's names, dga' ma (elsewhere Rati) and 'phrog byed (elsewhere Hari) are taken as epithets ("the Delighting Lady", "Graceful Lady of Hari"), and dbang phyug, Īśvara, appears in the feminine, Īśvarī. Several epithets are rendered literally because their Sanskrit originals are unclear, among them "Lord of Those Who Fly Upward", "Cutter of the Good", "the Piler", "the Threadless Lady", "the Escort" and "Garland of Pretence". As throughout this lexicon, interlinear headings (such as "One hundred and twelve names of Śakra" and "Names of Śakra's horse") are embedded in the verse lines and translated where they stand; the running counts are given as written. Ḥphrog byed, used of Indra here, is kept as Hari (Sanskrit hari also means a bay horse, Indra's steed). The names in the Nārada and Aśvin lists are largely literal renderings whose Sanskrit originals are uncertain (for example "Son of the Man-Giver", "the Mother's Mark", "the Aśvinī Dog"). "Names for nectar" falls inside the list of divine trees and seems to be a displaced heading for the preceding verse. This is a synonym lexicon whose transmitted text is interlarded with interlinear glosses and Sanskrit forms in Tibetan letters (for example "for fire there are one hundred and seven names," "for rākṣasas in general there are forty-two names," and the fragments inside the rākṣasa entry); these have been translated where they stand rather than removed. Several epithets of Agni, Yama and Rāvaṇa could not be securely matched to their Sanskrit originals and are rendered by meaning (e.g. Lhaji, the Slow-Holder, the Reckoner, Golden Sport). "Rider of the Goat" reads the sequence sgrub pa ra la zhon as Accomplisher plus Chāgaratha. In the list of flames, phur bu renders Sanskrit kīla, "flame," not a ritual dagger. The bracketed counts follow the text even where they do not match the number of items listed. The text is a synonym lexicon interspersed with interlinear glosses and with Sanskrit forms and grammatical remarks written in Tibetan letters, notably in the section on the directions; these remarks are translated as they stand, and their grammatical sense is at points uncertain. Several epithets of Varuṇa, Vāyu and Kubera could not be securely identified and are rendered by meaning. In the list of Kubera's names, ལུས་ངན ("Ugly Body") is the standard rendering of Kubera and is given so. In the list of the sky's names, སྒྲ་སྒྲོགས is rendered "the Resounding" rather than as the name Rāvaṇa, since it is an epithet of space here. The identifications of the elephants and cow-elephants of the directions follow the classical lists where the Tibetan clearly matches them and are otherwise rendered literally. The passage belongs to a synonym lexicon and preserves interlinear glosses and Sanskrit forms in Tibetan letters; these are translated where they stand, and many of the epithets of cloud, lightning, hail, fog and moon are rendered by meaning because their Sanskrit originals cannot be confidently identified. In the list of the moon's names, དབང་པོ renders Sanskrit indu, a name of the moon, and is given as Indu rather than Indra. The final verses list names of Dhruva, the pole star, and the mothers of various beings; the gloss "names for cold" stands misplaced in the text and is kept as it stands. The counts follow the text even where they do not match the items given. The passage is a synonym lexicon with interlinear glosses and Sanskrit forms written in Tibetan letters; these are translated where they stand. It opens with the names of the sage Agastya (the star Canopus), the Pot-Born who shrank the ocean, then gives the lunar mansions with their presiding deities, which are identified by their standard Sanskrit names, the planets, the seven sages, the zodiacal houses and the names of the sun. Many epithets, particularly of Agastya and the sun, could not be securely matched to Sanskrit and are rendered by meaning. The counts follow the text even where they do not match the items given. The passage belongs to a synonym lexicon interspersed with interlinear glosses (such as "names of the night" and "names for the new moon") and with Sanskrit forms in Tibetan letters; the glosses are translated where they stand. Many epithets of the sun's consorts, horses and charioteer (Aruṇa, the dawn) and of the night are rendered by meaning, since their Sanskrit originals cannot be securely identified. The months are given by their Sanskrit names, matched to the lunar mansions named in the Tibetan. The counts follow the text even where they do not match the items listed. This lexicon (a synonym dictionary of the Amarakośa type) has interlinear glosses that have entered the running text, such as "ten names for summer," "names of evil," "names of joy," "names of the self," "names of the day of the fortnight" and "names of mid-autumn." They are translated where they stand, and some sit in places where they do not fit the list they interrupt. The numerals after each group give the count of synonyms and are kept as given, even where they do not match the items. "Sak-born" (སག་སྐྱེས) could not be identified and is rendered literally. "The Head-born" probably refers to the months counted from Mārgaśīrṣa ("the Head"). In the list of auspicious words, ཞི་བ stands for Sanskrit śiva, "auspicious," and not the god. In "the festival of Smara," དྲན་པ means the god of love, not mindfulness. In "the retentive," གཟུངས means mental retention, not a dhāraṇī. "The clove" (ལི་ཤུ) in the list of words for mind is odd but is translated as written. The cosmological verses on the days of the hungry ghosts, gods, Brahmā and Manu are broken up by glosses and are translated as they stand. As in the earlier pieces, this is a synonym lexicon with counts after each group, and interlinear glosses ("names of fragrance," "names of white," "black," "names of colors") have entered the text. They are translated where they stand. In the list for liberation, ཞི་བ (śiva, "peaceful") and ཚངས་པ (brahman) are synonyms for liberation, not the gods Śiva and Brahmā. In "tradition (smṛti)," དྲན་པ is the body of remembered law, not Smara or mindfulness. In the list of colors, ཀ་དྲུ is the color term kadru, "tawny," not the goddess Kadrū. The line "for the stringing of sounds it is gumbyane" (སྒྲ་ཡི་མ་ལ་ནི་གུ་མྦྱ་ནེ) is uncertain. Some taste and smell terms ("the abundant," "the heart-descending smell") are rendered literally because they could not be identified. This is a synonym lexicon, and the numbers give the count of each group as the text has them. For the seven musical notes the Tibetan uses standard renderings (འཁོར་ཉན, ཁྱུ་མཆོག, དྲི་འཛིན, དྲུག་སྐྱེས, བར་མ, བློ་གསལ, ལྔ་པ). These are given as the Sanskrit niṣāda, ṛṣabha, gāndhāra, ṣaḍja, madhyama, dhaivata and pañcama. ཟླ་བ་ཅན, "moon-bearer," is taken as the peacock (candrakin). In "a deep sound is mantra," སྔགས most likely renders Sanskrit mandra, the low register, and was understood as mantra. A stray numeral "1" appears in the line on pañcama and is kept as "one." "Black" (ནག) before the reproach list looks like an interlinear gloss. As before, the numbers give the count of terms in each group as the text has them, and some do not match. The stray numeral in "the talker and the dancer, one" (གར ༡ པ) is kept as it stands. In the list of forms of address used in drama, "consecrated" (དབང་བསྐུར་བ) means a queen's royal consecration, not a tantric empowerment. The ten sentiments (rasa) include two terms, མཉེས་པ "the delightful" and ཤིན་པ "the intense," that could not be identified with confidence and are rendered literally. "Samādhi" among the divisions of the erotic sentiment is translated as written. In the list of terrible things, འཇིགས་བྱེད is the adjective "terrifying," not the name Vibhīṣaṇa. The phrase "aprahmaṇya is what is not to be done" (ཨ་པྲ་ཧྨ་ཎྱ་པ་སང་བྱ་མིན) is uncertain. This piece holds the colophon that closes the section on heaven, naming the text and its author, Śrīdharasena (དཔལ་འཛིན་སྡེ). It then opens the section on the underworld. The line "day and night, year, instant, laughter and festivals: as for these" breaks off before the colophon, and probably once gave their grammatical gender. ནོར་རྒྱས་བུ is taken as Vāsuki, by itself and as "king of serpents," following the parallel in the Amarakośa. Several serpent names are rendered literally because they could not be identified with confidence: "Horse-spittle," "the Brahmā serpent," "the Resonant," and "the Ass-haired one." ཀུ་དྲུ is read as Kadrū, mother of the nāgas. In "near fever," རིམས is not preceded by a word of rank or row, so it is read as fever. This is a synonym lexicon, so many entries are rare Sanskrit words merely transliterated in the Tibetan; these are given romanized as the Tibetan spells them. Several Tibetan calques could not be identified with confidence and are translated literally (for example the hollow-lier, the softener, the one born of the śagam, the bliss-binder). ལྟོ་འཕྱེ is rendered mahoraga per the house glossary, though in a list of snake synonyms it corresponds to Sanskrit uraga. བདེ་མཆོག་བྱེད in the list of words for water is taken as śaṃvara, a Sanskrit word for water, not the deity Cakrasaṃvara; likewise བདེ་བྱེད there is the common noun "bliss-maker", not the name Śaṃkara. ཁྱབ་འཇུག རྐང་པ འཇིགས་བྱེད་སྐྱེས is read as Viṣṇupadī and Bhīṣmasū, epithets of the Gaṅgā. This piece continues the lexicon's lists of words for water, banks, boats, fishing, fish and aquatic creatures. Many entries are rare Sanskrit words only transliterated in the Tibetan and are given romanized as spelled; many Tibetan calques could not be matched to a Sanskrit term with confidence and are translated literally. ཤ་ཟ here is a fish name, rendered "the flesh-eater" rather than as the spirit class piśāca. ཕུར་བུ here is read as a word for a fishing implement ("the peg"), not the planet. བདེ་བྱེད in "the donkey's bliss-maker" is a common noun, not the name Śaṃkara. This piece closes the lexicon's lists of aquatic creatures, wells, ponds and lotuses, and ends the first chapter. Many entries are Sanskrit words only transliterated in the Tibetan and are given romanized as spelled; Tibetan calques that could not be matched with confidence are translated literally, and two opaque ones (ཆུ་ཡི་རྔམ, ཆུའི་ལེན་བུ) are kept as "ngam" and "lenbu". གཞན་མཐའ་ཡས here means "the others are endless", not the name Ananta. འཕྲོག་བྱེད in the crab and frog lists is taken as the Sanskrit word hari, used here as a common noun. ཐུན་མཚམས is "twilight", not a ritual term. This piece opens the second chapter of the lexicon, on the earth, its kinds of ground, continents and lands. Many entries are Sanskrit words only transliterated in the Tibetan and are romanized as spelled; Tibetan calques of place names that could not be identified with confidence are translated literally. སྒྲ་ངན་ཞིང is read as Kurukṣetra and སྒྲ་མི་སྙན as Uttarakuru, standard Tibetan renderings. In the list of earth names, མཐའ་ཡས is the epithet Anantā, and རི་ཡི་ཕུར་བུ་མ is "pegged by mountains", not the planet Jupiter. སྐྱ་སྣར་བུ is read as Pāṭaliputra, མཉམ་ཡོད as Śrāvastī, and ལུས་འཕགས as Videha. This piece finishes the lexicon's section on the earth, with roads and distances, and begins the section on towns and buildings. Many entries are Sanskrit words only transliterated in the Tibetan and are romanized as spelled; calques that could not be matched with confidence are translated literally. འཕྲོག་བྱེད་ཟླ་གྲོང is read as Hariścandrapura, a name for the illusory city of the gandharvas. ཕུར་བུ here is "peg", not the planet, and དབྱུག་གུ is the door bar, not a unit of time. This is a synonym lexicon: each line lists words for one thing, and the numerals give the count of synonyms as the text states them; the counts do not always match the items and are kept as written. Sanskrit words merely transliterated in the Tibetan are given in romanized form as spelled. The phrases "thirty-one names for mountain" and "twenty-eight names for tree" appear to be interlinear glosses absorbed into the text; they are translated where they stand. In "the line" the text reads mtshar ("wonder"), evidently for tshar ("row, line"), which fits the list of words for "row". The words rendered "jubo", "tun", "the mountain's outpouring" (bud) and "the shelter it shows" (grag) are uncertain; the first two are left in their Tibetan form. The slow-flowing mountain may be Mandara, but this is not certain. A synonym lexicon: each run of words names one thing, and the numerals are the counts the text gives, kept as written even where they do not match the items. Words merely transliterated from Sanskrit are given romanized as spelled. Several phrases ("Names of the tree of awakening...", "These are names of the mango", "Names of the pomegranate", "Names of the campaka, the nāga and gold", "Names of the heap...", "Names of the very sour great juice") look like interlinear glosses absorbed into the text; they are translated where they stand. In "sahākara" the gloss divides the transliterated word sa-hākara (the fragrant mango); it is read as one word. In the citron list, "compassion" renders a word that stands for the Sanskrit plant name karuṇa. Phrases such as "names of the grape", "names of karavīra", "names of devadāru" and "names of the coral tree" look like interlinear glosses absorbed into the text; they are translated where they stand. The lists here describe, in order, among others the drumstick tree, soapnut, saptaparṇa, arjuna, birch, wood-apple, udumbara, śirīṣa, aguru, the three myrobalans, nimba, silk-cotton, cassia, kuṭaja, vetasa, lodhra, khadira and castor. A synonym lexicon of plants: the numerals are the counts the text gives, kept as written even where they do not match the items; words merely transliterated from Sanskrit are given romanized as spelled; the section turns from verse to prose partway through, and the translation follows it. This is a lexical list of plant synonyms; Tibetan calques are rendered by their meaning and Sanskrit words written in Tibetan letters are romanized as spelled. Several items are uncertain identifications: ལེ་བརྒན is taken as madder because the following synonyms (jiṅgī, the frog-leaved, the bright-leaved) are those of mañjiṣṭhā; མོན here renders Kirāta (kirātatikta). The numeral after the viḍaṅga list is written ༩༨ ("ninety-eight") though ten items are listed; it is translated as written. A lexical list of plant and drug synonyms in verse; Tibetan calques are rendered by meaning and Sanskrit words in Tibetan letters are romanized as spelled.
